Relating homotopy equivalences to conservativity in dependendent type theories with propositional computation
Matteo Spadetto

Abstract
We prove a conservativity result for extensional type theories over propositional ones, i.e. dependent type theories with propositional computation rules, using insights from homotopy type theory. The argument exploits a notion of canonical homotopy equivalence between contexts, and uses the notion of a category with attributes to phrase the semantics of theories of dependent types. Informally, our main result asserts that, for judgements essentially concerning h-sets, reasoning with extensional or propositional type theories is equivalent.



1
Introduction

In recent decades, dependent type theory has emerged as a powerful tool in the foundations of mathematics. Dependent types, i.e. types varying over the terms of other types, allow for precise and expressive specifications of mathematical statements and proofs, and provide a formal language for reasoning about them
[
NPS01
,
CH88
]
. This is not just of theoretical significance, as dependent types also have practical applications in fields such as software verification, where proof assistants like Coq and Agda have been successfully employed. More recently, the field has seen significant developments, based on the numerous insights provided by the foundational work of
Vladimir Voevodsky in univalent foundations. This includes the emergence of Homotopy Type Theory
[
Uni13
]
and the Univalent Foundations program
[
Voe15
]
, which provide a new understanding of the concept of equality. The focus of this paper is on the notion of conservativity between two different theories of dependent types, exploiting the insights coming from Homotopy Type Theory.

Suppose that we are given two dependent type theories
\textsc{T}_{1}
and
\textsc{T}_{2}
such that the inference rules, hence the type constructors, of
\textsc{T}_{1}
are contained (or inferable) in
\textsc{T}_{2}
. In other words, let us assume that
\textsc{T}_{2}
extends
\textsc{T}_{1}
. The first question that one can ask is whether
\textsc{T}_{1}
and
\textsc{T}_{2}
can deduce the same judgements, in which case the theories actually coincide (or are logically equivalent, respectively). However, even when this is not the case, one can still ask a question which is perhaps even more interesting: whether the two theories prove the same
statements
. By following the general interpretation of
statements as types
and
proofs as terms
(see
[
How80
,
ML84
,
Uni13
]
for more details), this property corresponds to the following: the theory
\textsc{T}_{1}
considers any given type inhabited, provided it is inhabited according to
\textsc{T}_{2}
, i.e. whenever
\textsc{T}_{2}
proves a term judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;t:T
, then
\textsc{T}_{1}
proves a term judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\tilde{t}:T
. When this happens, we say that
\textsc{T}_{2}
is
conservative
over
\textsc{T}_{1}
, meaning that
by “reinforcing” the theory
\textsc{T}_{1}
with the rules of
\textsc{T}_{2}
one does not risk to modify - namely increase - the deductive power of
\textsc{T}_{1}
.

The property of conservativity was studied by Martin Hofmann
[
Hof95a
,
Hof96
]
for an intensional type theory with
extensional concepts
as
\textsc{T}_{1}
and the extensional type theory for
\textsc{T}_{2}
. In this case a full conservativity result actually follows, but the additional extensional requirements on
\textsc{T}_{1}
- e.g. the identity proof irrelevance:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A,\;p:x=x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p=\textnormal{{r}}(x)

for paths in between a term and itself - are fundamental. In fact, a dependent type theory with fully intensional identity types does not make the type:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,y:A,\;p,q:x=y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p=q:\textsc{Type}

inhabited, while the extensional type theory does. This is the main topic of our work.

In this paper
we consider dependent type theory
T
, that we may call
propositional
, seen as a further weakening of a fully intensional dependent type theory, and we look for a concrete family of type judgements such that the extensional type theory is conservative over
T

relative
to that family, i.e. whenever the extensional type theory makes one of these types inhabited, so does
T
- see Theorem
6.4.1
. In detail, the theory
T
is going to be endowed with
propositional identity types
.

A dependent type theory is said to have
propositional identity types
if it is endowed with a type constructor consisting of the usual formation, introduction, and elimination rules of intensional identity type - see
Figure

2
- except for the judgemental equality of its computation rule. The latter is only required to hold in a weakened form, called
propositional form
, i.e. it is
replaced by a propositional equality
: whenever we are given judgements:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,y:A;\;p:x=y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;C(x,y,p):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;q(x):C(x,x,r(x))

and hence, by the elimination rule, a term judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,y:A;\;p:x=y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{J}}(q,x,y,p):C(x,y,p)
, then, in place of asking that the term equality judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{J}}(q,x,x,r(x))\equiv q(x)
holds, we only ask that it holds
propositionally
, i.e. that an additional term judgement of the form:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{H}}(q,x):\textnormal{{J}}(q,x,x,r(x))=q(x)

holds.

Cohen, Coquand, Danielsson, Huber, and Mörtberg
[
CD13
,
CCHM18
]
conducted initial analyses related to propositional identity types. Another work
[
BCH14
]
introduces a univalent model of
MLTT
where the propositional computation rule for identity types is validated, although its judgmental version is not. Following this, the type constructor has been thoroughly examined by van den Berg and Moerdijk
[
vdB18
,
vdBM18
]
, who introduced and explored a notion of semantics for dependent type theories with propositional identity types, using the notion of a
path category
. One may consider the same form of weakening for the computation rule of dependent sum types and dependent product types: these type constructors satisfying a propositional computation rule are called
propositional dependent sum types
and
propositional dependent product types
respectively. The dependent type theory
T
that we are going to consider is therefore a dependent type theory having propositional identity types, propositional dependent sum types, and propositional dependent product types with
function extensionality
in propositional form, together with an arbitrary family of atomic types and atomic terms. We call such a theory
propositional dependent type theory
. The aim of this paper is to provide a semantic proof that the corresponding extensional type theory is conservative over the propositional type theory
T
relative to the family of type judgements of the latter obtained by inductively applying the type formation rules to the atomic types that are provably h-sets in the latter.

The property of conservativity between various weakenings of extensional and intensional theories has been studied by several authors, in addition to the already cited Martin Hofmann
[
Hof96
]
. Bocquet
[
Boc20
]
examines the property of
Morita equivalence
between such a propositional theory and its extension obtained by strictifying the computation rule for identity types. Winterhalter, Sozeau, and Tabareau
[
WST19
]
, building on Hofmann’s point of view and on an approach devised by Oury
[
Our05
]
, define a translation from extensional theories to intensional ones with uniqueness of identity proofs and function extensionality. This translation is then adapted by Boulier and Winterhalter
[
BW19
,
Win20
]
to obtain one from extensional theories to propositional ones, the latter again with uniqueness of identity proofs and function extensionality. This adaptation is used to achieve a completely syntactical proof of the conservativity of the former over the latter.

The present paper can thus be seen as an alternative, semantic, approach to the same problem addressed by Boulier and Winterhalter, building on Hofmann’s argument. Our approach involves considering a propositional theory of dependent types, where dependent product types include function extensionality in propositional form, without initially requiring the uniqueness of identity proofs. The argument we follow gradually leads us to restrict the theory to contexts, called
h-elementary
, generated only from h-sets. Consequently, we end up with a result formulated more abstractly than the one contained in
[
BW19
,
Win20
]
, with a specific concrete case that essentially matches the latter. This approach also enables us to better understand both the strengths and limitations of Hofmann’s semantic argument by starting from a broader perspective. We also emphasise that the inductive notion of h-elementary type - see
Definition

5.0.1
- is not merely a technical tool that allows us to continue applying Hofmann’s argument, but it is also a natural and expressive family of statements to consider in a theory. For instance, all statements in Heyting arithmetic are h-elementary statements.

Structure of the paper.
In
Section

2
we recall the notion of category with attributes, that constitutes a notion of semantics for dependent type theories. We recall a well-known notion of
sound
semantics for extensional dependent type theories based on categories with attributes and we present an analogous one for propositional ones.

In
Section

3
we consider the notions of homotopy equivalence between types (namely
homotopy equivalence
) and of homotopy equivalence between contexts (
context homotopy equivalence
) in a propositional dependent type theory. These notions identify maps (between type and contexts, respectively) that are invertible up to homotopy (i.e. pointwise propositional equality and pointwise context propositional equality
[
GG08
]
respectively). We recall how to extend a homotopy equivalence between two contexts via a homotopy equivalence between two types in the given contexts, and how to obtain new homotopy equivalences between types, by starting from other given ones and applying the type constructors. Finally, we inductively define a family of
canonical
homotopy equivalences and a family of
canonical
context homotopy equivalences, notions appearing in other works
[
Hof95a
,
Hof96
,
Mai09
,
CM22
]
mentioned in
Section

3.6
. This notion is used in
Section

5
in order to make the syntax of a “sub-theory” of a propositional type theory into a model of an extensional type theory.

Section

4
is devoted to studying properties of the family of the canonical context homotopy equivalences. These properties are deduced by induction on the complexity of such an equivalence: as the family of the canonical context homotopy equivalences depends on the family of the canonical homotopy equivalences between types, a result for the former usually follows by induction starting from a result for the latter, which is obtained by induction as well. The properties of reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity hold for the general family of the canonical context homotopy equivalences. However, the fundamental property that any two parallel canonical equivalences are homotopic, i.e. pointwise context propositionally equal, does not hold, hence we need to restrict ourselves to the smaller family of canonical equivalences between those contexts that we call
contexts with h-propositional identities
(see Definition
4.2.2
). Having this property is fundamental to ensure the well-definedness of the composition in the category with attributes that we define in
Section

5
, starting from the syntax of the given propositional type theory. In detail, we use the property of
having h-propositional identities
at the end of the proof of Lemma
4.2.6
.

Section

5
defines a category with attributes
\bm{\mathfrak{C}}
starting from the
h-elementary
contexts of the given propositional type theory, identified up to canonical context homotopy equivalence. The h-elementary contexts are a special case of contexts with h-propositional identities that we define at the beginning of the section (see Definition
5.0.2
). This restriction makes the natural family of display maps that we define for
\bm{\mathfrak{C}}
into a
cartesian
natural transformation, so that we obtain a model of the strict substitution. Specifically, the h-elementariness property is used to deduce the uniqueness of the factorisation of a given square against a naturality square. We conclude our analysis by proving in
Section

6
that
\bm{\mathfrak{C}}
is actually a model of the extensional type theory. This allows us to infer, by soundness, the conservativity of the extensional theory over the h-elementary contexts of the propositional one.

1.1
Preliminary conventions and terminology

In this paper, a dependent type theory is intended to be
extensional
if it has extensional identity types, dependent product types, and dependent sum types, i.e. if it satisfies the rules of
Figure

1
,
Figure

4
, and
Figure

6
(see
Section

7
) respectively - or, for dependent sums, their equivalent formulation in
Figure

8
-. Analogously, a dependent type theory is said to be
propositional
if it has propositional identity types, propositional dependent product types with the propositional function extensionality rule, and propositional dependent sum types, i.e. if it satisfies the rules of
Figure

3
,
Figure

5
, and
Figure

7
respectively - or, for propositional dependent sums, their equivalent formulation in
Figure

9
-.

However, for sake of simplicity, for the remainder of the paper we consider
one
propositional type theory: i.e. we assume that we are given a dependent type theory, which is propositional, together with a family of atomic types and atomic terms and subject to the usual structural rules - for an enumeration see
[
Str91b
, Chapter III - context formation, context equality, judgement formation, judgement equality]
,
[
Jac99
]
,
[
Hof97
]
. We refer to this specific dependent type theory as Propositional Type Theory (
PTT
). Analogously, as there is only one extensional type theory that we actually consider in this paper (relative to the given
PTT
), i.e. the one whose atomic types are the atomic types of
PTT
that are provably
h-sets
in
PTT
(and whose atomic terms are the atomic terms of these atomic h-sets in
PTT
), we refer to it as Extensional Type Theory (
ETT
).

We use the symbology
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ -\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;-
to indicate judgements of
PTT
and the symbology
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ -\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;-
to indicate the ones of
ETT
. We use the symbol
\equiv
to indicate judgmental equalities between contexts, types in the same context and terms in the same context and of the same type. We use the symbol
=
to indicate propositional equalities i.e. identity types. Sometimes (especially in diagrams) we adopt the notations
x\Rightarrow y
and
x\Leftarrow y
, where
x\Rightarrow y
indicates the type
x=y
and
x\Leftarrow y
indicates the type
y=x
.

Sometimes, we use path induction on paths that may not appear to be general, i.e. paths of the form
x,y:A,p:x=y
for some type
A
. However, in these instances, we mean that the specific type on which we are performing path induction can be generalised so that the specific path
p^{\prime}
is replaced by a general path as
p
. This allows us to use path induction, after which we substitute
p^{\prime}
for the general path
p
in order to obtain the desired result. This argument is indicated as
generalised path induction
or
generalised path elimination
.

2
Recap on categories with attributes

This section is devoted to the notion of a
category with attributes
and its use to define an opportune notion of semantics for several kinds of dependent type theories. For further details, we refer the reader to
[
Car78
,
Mog91
,
KL21
]
.

Definition 2.0.1

(Category with attributes)
.

Suppose that we are given:

\succ

A category
\mathcal{C}
with terminal object
1
, whose objects are called
(semantic) contexts
.

\succ

A functor
\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{\textsc{tp}}\textsc{Discr}
, that we call
presheaf of (semantics) types
. If
\Gamma
is a semantic context, then an object
A
of the category
\textsc{tp}\Gamma
is said to be a
semantic type
in semantic context
\Gamma
. Here
Discr
denotes the category of (small) discrete categories.

\succ

A functor
\int\textsc{tp}\xrightarrow{-.-}\mathcal{C}
, that we call
(semantic) context extension
. Here
\int\textsc{tp}
denotes the Grothendieck construction associated to the presheaf
tp
.

\succ

A cartesian natural transformation:

\Big(\int\textsc{tp}\xrightarrow{-.-}\mathcal{C}\Big)\xrightarrow{P}\Big(\int\textsc{tp}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{C}\Big)

where
\pi
denotes the projection on the first component (we remind that a natural transformation is said to be
cartesian
when its naturality squares are pullbacks). The
(\Gamma,A)
-component:

\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{P_{A}}\Gamma

of
P
is called
display map
of
A
.

Then we say that the quadruple
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is a
category with attributes
.

The notion of category with attributes constitutes a notion of semantics for dependent type theories with the usual structural rules
[
Jac99
,
Hof97
]
[
Str91b
, Chapter III - context formation, context equality, judgement formation, judgement equality]
, hence with the usual
strict
notion of substitution. In this case, semantic terms of a category with attributes are presented as sections of the display maps. A modification of this concept, producing an equivalent notion of semantics, is used by other authors
[
CD14
,
Dyb96
,
Hof95a
,
Hof97
]
, where one requires that, for every semantic type
A
, a set of
semantic terms
is given. Other requirements imply that this set is in bijection with the sections of the corresponding display map
P_{A}
. In our case (see Remark
2.0.2
), the semantic terms of
A
are defined to be the the sections of
P_{A}
themselves: it turns out that our notion of category with attributes is the one of
[
Hof97
]
in the special case where the bijections between the semantic terms (of given semantic types) and the sections (of the corresponding display maps) are identities.

For an actually more general notion of semantics for dependent type theories we refer the reader to the notion of
comprehension category
, see
[
Jac93
]
, or to the one of
display map category
, see
[
Tay99
,
Jac99
,
MvG18
]
. In this case, in fact, a dependent type theory modelled by such a structure does not necessarily enjoy a strictly functorial notion of substitution, analogously to the case of locally cartesian closed categories (see
[
See84
]
) as pointed out by Hofmann
[
Hof95b
]
. For more details on a pseudo-functorial notion of substitution in dependent type theories, we refer the reader to
[
Cur93
]
.

Remark 2.0.2

(Notation and terminology)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
be a category with attributes.

\succ

For the
(\Gamma,A)
-component of
P
, we do not explicitly write the dependence on
\Gamma
. This does not generate ambiguity because, whenever we consider a semantic type, its semantic context is always specified.

\succ

If
f
is a morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\to\Gamma
, then we denote as
-[f]
the action:

\textsc{tp}\Gamma\to\textsc{tp}\Delta

of
tp
on
f
. Hence, if
A
is a semantic type in
\Gamma
, then
A[f]
denotes the action of
-[f]
on
A
. However, for sake of clarity we sometimes use the general notation
\textsc{tp}f
in place of
-[f]
, and hence we write
(\textsc{tp}f)A
in place of
A[f]
. E.g. this happens in
Sections

5
and
6
.

\succ

We remind that the objects of
\int\textsc{tp}
are pairs
(\Gamma,A)
, where
\Gamma
is a semantic context and
A
is semantic type in
\Gamma
. An arrow
(\Delta,B)\to(\Gamma,A)
of
\int\textsc{tp}
is a pair
(f,\varphi)
, where
f
is an arrow
\Delta\to\Gamma
and
\varphi
an arrow
B\to A[f]
. We refer to
[
MR13
]
for further details on this notion. The category
\textsc{tp}\Delta
is discrete, therefore an arrow
(\Delta,B)\to(\Gamma,A)
is nothing but a morphism of contexts
\Delta\to\Gamma
such that
B
is the type
A[f]
in
\Delta
. As we got rid of the dependence on the
\varphi
component, we denote the image of such an arrow via the functor
-.-
as:

\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{f.A}\Gamma.A

and any naturality (pullback, since
P
is cartesian) square of
P
is of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.A[f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A[f]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

for some semantic context morphism
f
.

\succ

Let
A
be a semantic type in semantic context
\Gamma
. The sections
\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A
of its display map
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{P_{A}}\Gamma
are called
(semantic) terms
of
A
in
\Gamma
.

In a given dependent type theory, whenever we are given contexts
\gamma:\Gamma
and
\delta:\Delta
, a type
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(\delta):\textsc{Type}
and a morphism of contexts
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f(\gamma):\Delta,B
(see the beginning of
Section

3
for both the notion of
morphism of contexts
and the meaning of this notation), then we implicitly mean that we are given a judgement of the form:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\overline{f}(\gamma):B(P_{B(\delta)}f(\gamma))

such that the substitution
f(\gamma):\Delta,B(\delta)
decomposes as:

P_{B(\delta)}f(\gamma):\Delta,\;\overline{f}(\gamma):B(P_{B(\delta)}f(\gamma))

where
P_{B(\delta)}
denotes the substitution
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,y:B(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\delta:\Delta
. Every category with attributes models this phenomenon:

Proposition 2.0.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
be a category with attributes. Let
f
be a semantic context morphism
\Gamma\to\Delta.B
, where
B
is a semantic type in
\Delta
. Then there is a unique semantic term
\overline{f}
of
B[P_{B}f]
in
\Gamma
such that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.B[P_{B}f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}f.B}

commutes. Moreover, if
f
is of the form:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.B[g]\xrightarrow{g.B}\Delta.B

for some semantic context morphism
\Gamma\xrightarrow{g}\Delta
and some semantic term
b
of
B[g]
, then the semantic term
\overline{f}
is
b
itself (observe that:

P_{B}f=P_{B}(g.B)b=gP_{B[g]}b=g

hence in this case
\Gamma.B[P_{B}f]=\Gamma.B[g]
, so that the equality
\overline{f}=b
typechecks).

Proof.

By cartesianity of
P
.
∎

2.1
Extending substitution to terms

In a category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
the notion of substitution for semantic types (given by the presheaf
tp
) naturally extends to semantic terms. Suppose that we are given a morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
, a semantic type
A
in
\Gamma
and a semantic term
a
of
A
. As the square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.A[f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A[f]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback and
P_{A}af=f
, there is a unique section
a[f]
of
P_{A[f]}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.A[f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\text{[}f\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}

commutes. We define the action of the substitution
-[f]
on
a
as the semantic term
a[f]
of
A[f]
. As expected:

Proposition 2.1.1
.

For a semantic term
a
of type
A
in context
\Gamma
and a semantic morphism
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
, the semantic terms
a[f]
and
\overline{af}
coincide.

Proof.

It holds that
af=(f.A)a[f]
. Hence by Proposition
2.0.3

\overline{af}
is
a[f]
.
∎

and that:

Proposition 2.1.2
.

For a semantic term
a
, the semantic term
a[f]
is functorial in
f
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.∎

We end the current subsection recalling the following:

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Suppose that
a
and
b
are semantic terms of some semantic type
A
in some semantic context
\Gamma
. Then:

a;b:=(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{a.A[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;)=(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;)

and
a;b
is the unique arrow
\Gamma\to\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]
whose postcompositions via
P_{A[P_{A}]}
and via
P_{A}.A
are
a
and
b
respectively. Moreover, if
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
is a morphism of contexts, then:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a[f];b[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a;b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A.A[P_{A}]}

commutes.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

Remark 2.1.4
.

If we are given a dependent type theory, if we are given judgements of the form
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):A(\gamma)
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;b(\gamma):A(\gamma)
, then the morphism of semantic contexts
a;b
of Lemma
2.1.3
corresponds to the morphism of contexts
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\gamma,a(\gamma),b(\gamma)
.

2.2
Variable terms

Let
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
be a category with attributes and let
A
be a semantic type in a semantic context
\Gamma
. Then, we might consider the semantic term
\overline{1_{\Gamma.A}}
of
A[P_{A}]
, that is a section:

\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{\overline{1_{\Gamma.A}}}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]

of
P_{A[P_{A}]}
. We denote this semantic term as
v_{A}
and call it
semantic variable
of
A
and remind that it is characterised, among the sections of
P_{A[P_{A}]}
, as the one satisfying
(P_{A}.A)v_{A}=1_{\Gamma.A}
. We recall, without proof, some important equalities:

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
be an arrow in
\mathcal{C}
and consider the corresponding extension
\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{f.A}\Gamma.A
. Then the equality:

v_{A}[f.A]=v_{[A[f]]}

between semantic terms of
A[P_{A}(f.A)]=A[fP_{A[f]}]
holds.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

If
a
is a semantic term of
A
, then:

(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{v_{A}}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;)=(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{a.A[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;).

In particular, the equality
v_{A}[a]=a
between sections of
P_{A}
holds.

Remark 2.2.3
.

In a given dependent type theory, if
\gamma:\Gamma
is a context and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
is a type, then two morphisms of contexts naturally arise:

\displaystyle{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x:A(\gamma)

\displaystyle{\penalty\displaystyle\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\gamma:\Gamma

\displaystyle{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x:A(\gamma)

\displaystyle{\penalty\displaystyle\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;x:A(\gamma)\equiv A(P_{A(\gamma)}(\gamma,x)).

The former corresponds to the morphism of contexts
P_{A}
in a given category with attributes, while the latter, seen as a term rather then a general morphism of contexts, to the semantic term
v_{A}
associated to a given semantic type
A
.

2.3
Semantics of extensional and propositional type theories

In this subsection we briefly describe the additional structure that a category with attributes needs to be equipped with in order to allow on it an interpretation of an extensional type theory or of a propositional one.

The following two notions,
Definition

2.3.1
and
Definition

2.3.2
, define when a category with attributes is equipped with semantic identity types (in the extensional and propositional case, respectively). In detail, the following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the extensional identity types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation and introduction

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;x=x^{\prime}:\textsc{Type}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{r}}(x):x=x}

Extensionality and identity proof irrelevance

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A;\;p:x=x^{\prime}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;x\equiv x^{\prime}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A;\;p:x=x^{\prime}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p\equiv\textnormal{{r}}(x)}

referring the reader to
Figure

1
for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.1

(Semantic extensional identity types)
.

We say that a category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic extensional identity types
if, for every semantic context
\Gamma
and every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, there is a choice of:

\succ

(
Formation
) a semantic type
\textnormal{{id}}_{A}
of semantic context
\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]
;

\succ

(
Introduction
) a morphism of contexts:

\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{r}}_{A}}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}

such that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{A}}

commutes;

in such a way that the following properties are satisfied:

\succ

(
Extensionality
) For all semantic terms
a
and
b
of semantic type
A
and every semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{p}\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}(a,b)
of semantic type:

\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;b]=\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{a.A[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;]=\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;]

in context
\Gamma
, the equality
a=b
between semantic terms of type
A
and the equality:

(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{p}\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;b]=\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;a]\;)=(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{r}}_{A}^{a}}\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;a]\;)

between semantic terms of type
\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;a]
hold. Here
\textnormal{{r}}_{A}^{a}
is the unique arrow
\Gamma\dashrightarrow\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;a]
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[a;a]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.A[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\textnormal{{id}}_{A}(a,a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((a.A[P_{A}])a).\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}\;=\;a;a.\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

commutes (observe that
P_{\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}\textnormal{{r}}_{A}a=v_{A}a=(a.A[P_{A}])a
by Lemma
2.2.2
).

\succ

(
Compatibility with the substitution
) If
f
is a morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\to\Gamma
then the equality:

\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[\;\Delta.A[f].A[fP_{A[f]}]\xrightarrow{f.A.A[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;]=\textnormal{{id}}_{A[f]}

between semantic types in context
\Delta.A[f].A[fP_{A[f]}]
holds and:

\textnormal{{r}}_{A}[f]=\textnormal{{r}}_{A[f]}

where
\textnormal{{r}}_{A}[f]
is the unique arrow
\Delta.A[f]\to\Delta.A[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A[f]}
such that
P_{\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}\textnormal{{r}}_{A}[f]=v_{A}
and such that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A[f]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}_{A}[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}

commutes.

The following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the propositional identity types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation and introduction

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;x=x^{\prime}:\textsc{Type}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{r}}(x):x=x}

Path elimination (i.e. path induction) and propositional computation

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A;\;p:x=x^{\prime}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;C(x,x^{\prime},p):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;q(x):C(x,x,\textnormal{{r}}(x))}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x,x^{\prime}:A;\;p:x=x^{\prime}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{J}}(q,x,x^{\prime},p):C(x,x^{\prime},p)\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{H}}(q,x):\textnormal{{J}}(q,x,x,\textnormal{{r}}(x))=q(x)}

referring the reader to Figure
3
for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.2

(Semantic propositional identity types)
.

We say that a category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic propositional identity types
if it satisfies
formation
,
introduction
,
compatibility with the substitution
of Definition
2.3.1
and moreover:

\succ

(
Elimination and propositional computation
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}
and every semantic term
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{c}\Gamma.A.C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]
of
C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]
in context
\Gamma.A
, there is a choice of a semantic term:

\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{J}}_{c}}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}.C

of type
C
in context
\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}
and of a semantic term:

\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{H}}_{c}}\Gamma.A.\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]}[\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}];c]

of type
\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]}[\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}];c]
in context
\Gamma.A
;

\succ

(
Additional compatibility with the substitution
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}
, every semantic term
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{c}\Gamma.A.C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]
of
C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]
in context
\Gamma.A
and every morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
, the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[fP_{A[f]}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A[f]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[f.A.A[P_{A}]]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[fP_{A[f]}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A[f]}.C[f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}[f.A.A[P_{A}]].C[f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{J}}_{\big(\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{c[f.A]}\Delta.A[f].C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}(f.A)]=\Delta.A[f].C[(f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A})\textnormal{{r}}_{A[f]}]\big)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}]}

commutes i.e. the equality
\textnormal{{J}}_{c[f.A]}=\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}]
holds, and that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[(f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A})\textnormal{{r}}_{A[f]}]}[\;\textnormal{{J}}_{c[f.A]}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A[f]}];c[f.A]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}][f.A]}[\;\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[f.A.A[P_{A}].\textnormal{{id}}_{A}][\textnormal{{r}}_{A[f]}];c[f.A]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}][f.A]}[\;\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}][f.A];c[f.A]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].(\;\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]}[\;f.A.C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}].C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}P_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]}]\;]\;)[\;\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}][f.A];c[f.A]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].(\;\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}]}[\textnormal{{J}}_{c}[\textnormal{{r}}_{A}];c]\;)[f.A]}}
Lemma
2.1.3
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{H}}_{c}[f.A]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{H}}_{c[f.A]}}

commutes i.e. the equality
\textnormal{{H}}_{c[f.A]}=\textnormal{{H}}_{c}[f.A]
holds.

The following two notions (Definition
2.3.3
and Definition
2.3.4
) define when a category with attributes is equipped with semantic dependent product types (in the extensional and propositional case, respectively). In detail, the following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the dependent product types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation and elimination

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\Pi_{x:A}B(x):\textsc{Type}}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z:\Pi_{x:A}B(x);\;x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x):B(x)}

Introduction,
\beta
-reduction,
\eta
-expansion

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;y(x):B(x)}{{\lambda x.y(x):\Pi_{x:A}B(x)}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{ev}}(\lambda x.y(x),x)\equiv y(x)}\par\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z:\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;z\equiv\lambda x.\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)}

referring the reader to
Figure

4
for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.3

(Semantic dependent product types)
.

We say that a category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic dependent product types
if:

\succ

(
Formation
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
and every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, there is a choice of a semantic type
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
;

\succ

(
Introduction
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
and every semantic term
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A.B
of
B
, there is a choice of a semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\lambda b}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
of
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
;

\succ

(
Elimination
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{z}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
of
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
and every semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A
of
A
, a choice of a semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{ev}}_{z}^{a}}\Gamma.B[a]
of
B[a]
;

in such a way that the following properties are satisfied:

\succ

(
Compatibility with the substitution
) For every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
and every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, and for every choice of a semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{z}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
of
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
, of a semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A
of
A
and of a semantic term
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A.B
of
B
, if
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
is a morphism of semantic contexts, then:

–

the equality:

\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}[f]=\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}

between semantic types in context
\Gamma
holds;

–

the equality:

\bigg(\textnormal{{ev}}_{\Gamma\xrightarrow{z}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}^{\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A}\bigg)[f]=\bigg(\textnormal{{ev}}_{\Gamma\xrightarrow{z[f]}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}^{\Gamma\xrightarrow{a[f]}\Gamma.A[f]}\bigg)

between semantic terms of type
B[\;\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\;]=B[\;\Delta\xrightarrow{a[f]}\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{f.A}\Gamma.A\;]
holds;

–

the equality:

(\;\lambda(\;\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A.B\;)\;)[f]=\lambda(\;\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{b[f.A]}\Delta.A[f].B[f.A]\;)

between semantic terms of type
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}[f]=\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}
holds.

\succ

(
\beta
-reduction
) If
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A.B
is a semantic term of
B
and if
\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A
is a semantic term of
A
, for some semantic context
\Gamma
, some semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
and some semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, then the equality:

(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a}}\Gamma.B[a]\;)=(\;\Gamma\xrightarrow{b[a]}\Gamma.B[a]\;)

between semantic terms of type
B[a]
holds.

\succ

(
\eta
-expansion
) If
\Gamma\xrightarrow{z}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
is a semantic term of type
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
, then the equality:

\lambda(\;\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{\Bigg(\textnormal{{ev}}_{\big(\;\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{z[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}[P_{A}]=\Gamma.A.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[P_{A}]}^{B[P_{A}.A]}\;\big)}^{\big(\;\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{v_{A}}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]\;\big)}\Bigg)}\Gamma.A.B[\;(P_{A}.A)v_{A}\;]=\Gamma.A.B\;)=z

between semantic terms of type
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
holds.

The following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the propositional dependent product types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation, elimination, propositional
\Pi
-extensionality

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\Pi_{x:A}B(x):\textsc{Type}}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z:\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(x);\;x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x):B(x)}\par\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z,z^{\prime}:\Pi_{x:A}B(x);\;x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p(z,z^{\prime},x):\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)=\textnormal{{ev}}(z^{\prime},x)}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z,z^{\prime}:\Pi_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\rho(p,z,z^{\prime}):z=z^{\prime}}

Introduction, propositional
\beta
-reduction, propositional
\eta
-expansion

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;y(x):B(x)}{{\lambda x.y(x):\Pi_{x:A}B(x)}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\beta^{\Pi}(y,x):\textnormal{{ev}}(\lambda x.y(x),x)=y(x)}\par\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ z:\Pi_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\eta^{\Pi}(z):z=\lambda x.\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)}

referring the reader to
Figure

5
and for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.4

(Semantic propositional dependent product types)
.

We say that a category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic propositional dependent product types
if it is equipped with semantic propositional identity types, it satisfies
formation
,
introduction
,
elimination
,
compatibility with the substitution
of Definition
2.3.3
and moreover:

\succ

(
Propositional
\beta
-reduction and additional compatibility with the substitution
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic term
\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b}\Gamma.A.B
of
B
and every semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A
of
A
, there is a choice of a semantic term:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{\beta_{b}^{a}}\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[a]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a};b[a]\;]

of type
\textnormal{{id}}_{B[a]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a};b[a]\;]
, in such a way that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[a]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a};b[a]\;][f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[a]}[\;f.B[a].B[aP_{B[a]}]\;][\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a}[f];b[a][f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[a][f]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a}[f];b[a][f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[f.A][a[f]]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda b}^{a}[f];b[f.A][a[f]]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[f.A][a[f]]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{(\lambda b)[f]}^{a[f]};b[f.A][a[f]]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{B[f.A][a[f]]}[\;\textnormal{{ev}}_{\lambda(b[f.A])}^{a[f]};b[f.A][a[f]]\;]}}
Lemma
2.1.3
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{b}^{a}[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{b[f.A]}^{a[f]}}

commutes i.e.
\beta_{b}^{a}[f]=\beta_{b[f.A]}^{a[f]}
, for every morphism of contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
;

\succ

(
Propositional
\eta
-expansion and additional compatibility with the substitution
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
and every semantic term
\Gamma\xrightarrow{z}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
of
\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}
, there is a choice of a semantic term:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{\eta_{z}}\Gamma.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}[\;\lambda\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}};z\;]

of type
\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}[\;\lambda\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}};z\;]
, in such a way that the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}[\;\lambda\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}};z\;][f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}[\;f.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}.\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}[P_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}}]\;][\;(\lambda\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}})[f];z[f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A}^{B}[f]}[\;(\lambda\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}})[f];z[f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}[\;\lambda(\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}]}^{v_{A}}[f.A]);z[f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}[\;\lambda(\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[P_{A}][f.A]}^{v_{A}[f.A]});z[f]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\textnormal{{id}}_{\mathbf{{\Pi}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}[\;\lambda(\textnormal{{ev}}_{z[f][P_{A[f]}]}^{v_{A[f]}});z[f]\;]}}
Lemma
2.1.3
Lemma
2.2.1
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{z}[f]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{z[f]}}

commutes i.e.
\eta_{z}[f]=\eta_{z[f]}
, for every morphism of contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
.

The following two notions (Definition
2.3.5
and Definition
2.3.6
) define when a category with attributes is equipped with semantic dependent sum types (in the extensional and propositional case, respectively). In detail, the following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the dependent sum types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation and introduction

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\Sigma_{x:A}B(x):\textsc{Type}}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A,y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\langle x,y\rangle:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)}

Elimination and computation

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ u:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;C(u):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A;\;y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;c(x,y):C(\langle x,y\rangle)}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ u:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{split}}(c,u):C(u)\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A;\;y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{split}}(c,\langle x,y\rangle)\equiv c(x,y)}

referring the reader to
Figure

6
for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.5

(Semantic dependent sum types)
.

A category with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic dependent sum types
if:

\succ

(
Formation and introduction
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
and every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, there is a choice of a semantic type
\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
and of a morphism of contexts
\Gamma.A.B\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}}\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}}

commutes;

\succ

(
Elimination
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
and every semantic term
c
of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
in context
\Gamma.A.B
, there is a choice of a semantic term
\textnormal{{split}}_{c}
of type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
;

in such a way that the following properties are satisfied:

\succ

(
Computation
) For every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
and every semantic term
c
of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
in context
\Gamma.A.B
, the equality:

(\;\Gamma.A.B\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}\Gamma.A.B.C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]\;)=(\;\Gamma.A.B\xrightarrow{c}\Gamma.A.B.C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]\;)

between semantic terms of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
holds.

\succ

(
Compatibility with the substitution
) For every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
, every semantic term
c
of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
in context
\Gamma.A.B
, if
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
is a morphism of semantic contexts, then:

–

the equality:

\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}[f]=\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}

between semantic types in context
\Gamma
holds;

–

the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A.B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}}

commutes;

–

the equality between the semantic terms:

\displaystyle\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}[f]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}]}\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}[f].C[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}]

\displaystyle\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\textnormal{{split}}_{c[f.A.B]}}\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}.C[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}]

of semantic type
C[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}]
in semantic context
\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}[f]=\Delta.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}
holds, where we remind that
c[f.A.B]
is a term:

\Delta.A[f].B[f.A]\;\to\;\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\big(\;C\big[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}(f.A.B)\big]\;=\;C\big[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}\big]\big[\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}\big]\;\big).

The following notion is meant to model the inference rules for the propositional dependent sum types, that here we recall in a concise form:

Formation and introduction

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}}{{\Sigma_{x:A}B(x):\textsc{Type}}\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A,y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\langle x,y\rangle:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)}

Elimination and propositional computation

\frac{{A:\textsc{Type}}\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(x):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ u:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;C(u):\textsc{Type}\quad\quad{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A;\;y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;c(x,y):C(\langle x,y\rangle)}{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ u:\Sigma_{x:A}B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{split}}(c,u):C(u)\\
\\
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ x:A;\;y:B(x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\sigma(c,x,y):\textnormal{{split}}(c,\langle x,y\rangle)=c(x,y)}

referring the reader to
Figure

7
for the extended version.

Definition 2.3.6

(Semantic propositional dependent sum types)
.

We say that
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
equipped with semantic propositional dependent sum types
if it is equipped with semantic propositional identity types, it satisfies
formation and introduction
,
elimination
,
compatibility with the substitution
of Definition
2.3.5
and moreover:

\succ

(
Propositional computation
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
and every semantic term
c
of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
in context
\Gamma.A.B
, there is a choice of a semantic term:

\Gamma.A.B\xrightarrow{\sigma_{c}}\Gamma.A.B.\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}];c\;]

of type
\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}];c\;]
;

\succ

(
Additional compatibility with the substitution
) for every semantic context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
A
in context
\Gamma
, every semantic type
B
in context
\Gamma.A
, every semantic type
C
in context
\Gamma.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}
, every semantic term
c
of type
C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]
in context
\Gamma.A.B
and every morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
, the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}][\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c[f.A.B]}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}];c[f.A.B]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}][f.A.B]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c[f.A.B]}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}];c[f.A.B]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}][f.A.B]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[f.\mathbf{{\Sigma}}_{A}^{B}][\textnormal{{p}}_{A[f]}^{B[f.A]}];c[f.A.B]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}][f.A.B]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}][f.A.B];c[f.A.B]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}[\;f.A.B.C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}].C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}P_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}]\;][\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}][f.A.B];c[f.A.B]\;]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].B[f.A].\textnormal{{id}}_{C[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}]}[\;\textnormal{{split}}_{c}[\textnormal{{p}}_{A}^{B}];c\;][f.A.B]}}
Lemma
2.1.3
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{c[f.A.B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{c}[f.A.B]}

commutes i.e.
\sigma_{c[f.A.B]}=\sigma_{c}[f.A.B]
.

We have all the notions that we need to give the following:

Definition 2.3.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
be a category with attributes.

\succ

If
T
is a given extensional type theory, we say that
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
model of
T
if it is equipped with semantic extensional identity types, with semantic dependent product types, with semantic dependent sum types and with a choice of a semantic type (and of a semantic term) in context
1
for every atomic type (and every atomic term) of
T
.

\succ

If
T
is a given propositional type theory, we say that
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
is
model of
T
if it is equipped with semantic propositional identity types, with semantic propositional dependent product types, with semantic propositional dependent sum types and with a choice of a semantic type (and of a semantic term) in context
1
for every atomic type (and every atomic term) of
T
.

Definition 2.3.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{D},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{D}})
be categories with attributes. A
morphism of categories with attributes
:

F:(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{C}})\to(\mathcal{D},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{D}})

consists of:

\succ

a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
preserving the terminal object;

\succ

a natural transformation, that we continue calling
F
, from
\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}}F
, i.e. the equality:

F(A[f])=(FA)[Ff]

holds for every semantic type
A
in some semantic context
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{C}
and every morphism of semantic contexts
\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
of
\mathcal{C}
;

in such a way that the equality:

F(-.-)=(F-).(F-)

holds and that
FP_{A}=P_{FA}
for every semantic type
A
in some semantic context
\Gamma
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Moreover, if
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{C}})
and
(\mathcal{D},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{D}})
are models of a given extensional (propositional, respectively) type theory
T
, then a
morphism of
T
:

(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{C}})\to(\mathcal{D},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{D}})

is a morphism of categories with attributes
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{C}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{C}})\to(\mathcal{D},\textsc{tp}_{\mathcal{D}},-.-,P_{\mathcal{D}})
preserving the semantic extensional (propositional, respectively) identity types, the semantic (propositional, respectively) dependent product types, the semantic (propositional, respectively) dependent sum types and the choice of the semantic types (and of the semantic term) in context
1
.

Remark 2.3.9
.

The one that we presented in Definition
2.3.8
happens to be the
strict
notion of morphism between categories with attributes, and it is considered e.g. by Cartmell
[
Car78
]
and by Kapulkin and Lumsdaine
[
KL21
]
. In this paper we only deal with morphisms between categories with attributes in this strict form: they all strictly commute with the given semantic context extensions and the display maps. The syntactic model of an extensional (propositional) type theory (see Remark
2.3.11
) enjoys a strict universal initiality property with respect to morphisms of the given existential (propositional, respectively) type theory in this strong form.

However several weakenings of this notion are available in the literature, depending on their strictness in the commutativity with the category with attributes structure. E.g. the notion used by Clairambault and Dybjer
[
CD14
]
preserves the semantic context extension only up to natural isomorphism. We refer the reader to
[
New18
]
for more details (regarding in this case the related structure of natural model).

The propositional type theory
hPTT
.
Now, let us consider the given
PTT
(see
Section

1.1
). Let us consider the propositional type theory contained in
PTT
(meaning that all of its contexts, types, terms and judgements are contexts, types, terms and judgements of
PTT
) whose atomic types are the ones of
PTT
that are provably h-sets in
PTT
(and whose atomic terms are the atomic terms of these atomic h-sets in
PTT
). We indicate this specific propositional type theory as
hPTT
, since the contexts of
hPTT
are the ones that we call
homotopy elementary
contexts of
PTT
(see Definition
5.0.2
).

Remark 2.3.10
.

Since
ETT
and
hPTT
have the same atomic types and terms (see
Section

1.1
), every model of
ETT
is canonically a model of
hPTT
: in order to obtain a choice of the terms
\textnormal{{J}}_{c}
,
\textnormal{{H}}_{c}
,
\beta_{b}^{a}
,
\eta_{z}
and
\sigma_{c}
, one defines them as instances of
\textnormal{{r}}_{T}^{t}
for opportune semantic types
T
and semantic terms
t
. Then, all of the additional compatibilities with the substitution follow in fact by the one of
\textnormal{{r}}_{T}
.

With this choice of a structure of model of
hPTT
for every model of
ETT
, every morphism of
ETT
is canonically a morphism of
hPTT
.

Remark 2.3.11
.

The category whose objects are the contexts of
ETT
and whose arrows are the morphisms of contexts (identified up to
renaming their free variables and up to componentwise equality judgement) has for terminal object the empty context and constitutes a category with attributes with the following data:

\succ

the semantic types in a given semantic context
\gamma:\Gamma
are the type judgements
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
of
ETT
in context
\gamma
; the presheaf of semantic types act on the morphisms of contexts by substitution;

\succ

the semantic context extension maps a pair
(\gamma:\Gamma,{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type})
to the context:

\gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)

and an arrow
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;f(\delta):\Gamma
of target
(\gamma:\Gamma,{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type})
to the morphism of contexts:

\delta,x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\;\;{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(f(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;f(\delta):\Gamma,x^{\prime}:A(f(\delta))\;\;}\gamma,x;

\succ

whenever
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
is a semantic type in semantic context
\gamma
i.e. a type judgement in context
\gamma
, then the display map
P_{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;T(\gamma):\textsc{Type}}
is the morphism of contexts:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;\gamma;

its sections - i.e. the semantic terms of
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
- are the morphisms of contexts
\gamma\to\gamma,x
of the form:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;\gamma,a(\gamma):A(\gamma)

for some term judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):A(\gamma)
.

We indicate as
\mathbf{eTT}
this category with attributes. Then
\mathbf{eTT}
is a model of
ETT
with the clear choices of the semantic extensional identity types, of the semantic dependent sum types, and of the semantic dependent product types. The choice of a semantic type (and of a semantic term) in empty context for every atomic type (and every atomic term) is the identity.

Analogously,
PTT
and
hPTT
form models of themselves, which we indicate as
\mathbf{pTT}
and
\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}
.

Theorem 2.3.12

(Soundness)
.

For every model
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
of
ETT
, there is unique a morphism
\mathbf{eTT}\to(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
of
ETT
.

Analogously, for every model
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
of
PTT
, there is unique a morphism:

\mathbf{pTT}\to(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)

of
PTT
and, for every model
(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)
of
hPTT
, there is unique a morphism:

\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}\to(\mathcal{C},\textsc{tp},-.-,P)

of
hPTT
.

We conclude this subsection by noticing the following
Remark

2.3.13
. For further details on this general notion of semantics (or equivalent ones) for dependent type theories, we refer the reader to
[
Car78
,
Pit00
,
Str91a
]
.

Remark 2.3.13
.

By Remark
2.3.10
and by Theorem
2.3.12
, there is unique a morphism:

\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}\to\mathbf{eTT}

of
hPTT
. We call it
canonical interpretation
of
hPTT
into
ETT
and denote it as
|\cdot|
. A priori, being a morphism of
hPTT
, the mapping
|\cdot|
is defined on contexts, morphisms of contexts and type judgements: we now show how to extend the mapping
|\cdot|
to types and terms in context.

Let
\gamma:\Gamma
and let
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
be a type judgement of
hPTT
in context
\gamma
(i.e. an
h-elementary
type judgement of
PTT
in
h-elementary
context
\gamma
- see Definition
5.0.1
and Definition
5.0.2
). As
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
is a semantic type in semantic context
\gamma
in
\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}
, then
|{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}|
needs to be a semantic type in semantic context
|\gamma|
in
\mathbf{eTT}
i.e. a type judgement of
ETT
in context
|\gamma|
. Hence
|{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}|
is of the form:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|A(\gamma)|:\textsc{Type}

where
|A(\gamma)|
denotes therefore a type of
ETT
in context
|\gamma|
.

Let
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):A(\gamma)
be a term judgement of
hPTT
. Then the morphism
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\gamma:\Gamma,a(\gamma):A(\gamma)
is a section of
P_{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}}
in
\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}
and therefore
|{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\gamma:\Gamma,a(\gamma):A(\gamma)|
is a section
|\gamma|\to|\gamma|,|x|:|A(\gamma)|
of:

|P_{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}}|=P_{|{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}|}=P_{{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|A(\gamma)|:\textsc{Type}}={\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|,|x|:|A(\gamma)|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|\gamma|

in
\mathbf{eTT}
. Hence
|{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\gamma:\Gamma,a(\gamma):A(\gamma)|
needs to be of the form:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|\gamma|,|a(\gamma)|:|A(\gamma)|

for a term judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|a(\gamma)|:|A(\gamma)|
, where
|a(\gamma)|
denotes therefore a term of
ETT
in context
|\gamma|
and of type
|A(\gamma)|
.

If
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f(\delta):\Gamma
is a morphism of contexts of
\textnormal{{h}}\mathbf{pTT}
and if we write
|A|(|\gamma|)
and
|a|(|\gamma|)
for
|A(\gamma)|
and
|a(\gamma)|
-respectively- in context
|\gamma|
, we observe that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|A(f(\delta))|\equiv|A|(|f(\delta)|)
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ |\gamma|\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}_{\textnormal{{ext}}}\;\;\;|a(f(\delta))|=|a|(|f(\delta)|)

because
|\cdot|
needs to commute with the substitution.

3
Homotopy equivalences of contexts in Propositional Type Theory

This section mostly deals with the notion of morphism of contexts and the concept of generalised identity type of two parallel morphisms of context. Here we briefly recap these notions as long as we need them. For more details, we refer the reader to
[
GG08
]
.

Suppose that we are given two contexts
\gamma:\Gamma
and
\delta:\Delta
of
PTT
, where the former is an abbreviation for the list
\gamma_{1}:\Gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:\Gamma_{2}(\gamma_{1}),...,\gamma_{n}:\Gamma_{n}(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{n-1})
and the latter for
\delta_{1}:\Delta_{1},\delta_{2}:\Delta_{2}(\delta_{1}),...,\delta_{m}:\Delta_{m}(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{m-1})
. With the expression
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):\Delta
we mean a list of judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{1}(\gamma):\Delta_{1}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{2}(\gamma):\Delta_{2}(a_{1}(\gamma))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{3}(\gamma):\Delta_{3}(a_{1}(\gamma),a_{2}(\gamma))

\succ

…

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{m}(\gamma):\Delta_{m}(a_{1}(\gamma),a_{2}(\gamma),a_{3}(\gamma),...,a_{m-1}(\gamma))

and we call such a list a
morphism of contexts

\gamma\to\delta
. Now, suppose that
\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
indicates the context:

\delta_{1}:\Delta_{1},...,\delta_{m-1}:\Delta_{m-1}(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{m-2})

and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a^{\prime}(\gamma):\Delta^{\prime}
indicates the list:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{1}(\gamma):\Delta_{1}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{2}(\gamma):\Delta_{2}(a_{1}(\gamma))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{3}(\gamma):\Delta_{3}(a_{1}(\gamma),a_{2}(\gamma))

\succ

…

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a_{m-1}(\gamma):\Delta_{m}(a_{1}(\gamma),a_{2}(\gamma),a_{3}(\gamma),...,a_{m-2}(\gamma))

and
A(\delta^{\prime})
indicates the type in context
\Delta_{m}(\delta_{1},...,\delta_{m-1})
. Then we may also write anyone of the following:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):\Delta^{\prime},A\text{ \, \, \, }{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a^{\prime}(\gamma),a_{m}(\gamma):\Delta

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a^{\prime}(\gamma),a_{m}(\gamma):\Delta^{\prime},A\text{ \, \, \, }{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a^{\prime}(\gamma):\Delta^{\prime},a_{m}(\gamma):A(a^{\prime}(\gamma))

in order to indicate the same morphism of contexts
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):\Delta
.

If we are given two parallel morphisms of contexts
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;a(\gamma):\Delta
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;b(\gamma):\Delta
, the expression
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p(\gamma):a(\gamma)=b(\gamma)
indicates the list:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p_{1}(\gamma):a_{1}(\gamma)=b_{1}(\gamma)

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p_{2}(\gamma):a_{2}(\gamma)=p_{1}(\gamma)^{*}b_{2}(\gamma)

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p_{3}(\gamma):a_{3}(\gamma)=(p_{1}(\gamma),p_{2}(\gamma))^{*}b_{3}(\gamma)

\succ

…

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p_{m}(\gamma):a_{m}(\gamma)=(p_{1}(\gamma),...,p_{m-1}(\gamma))^{*}b_{m}(\gamma)

where the operations
(p_{1}(\gamma),...,p_{k}(\gamma))^{*}
are defined by sequential (generalised) path inductions on
p_{k}
,
p_{k-1}
, …,
p_{2}
, and
p_{1}
and hence make the identity types:

(\;\textnormal{{r}}(a_{1}(\gamma)),...,\textnormal{{r}}(a_{k}(\gamma))\;)^{*}d=d

inhabited (remind that
PTT
has
propositional
identity types) if
d:\Delta_{k+1}(b_{1}(\gamma),...,b_{k}(\gamma))
. We call
context propositional equality
this new meaning of the symbol
=
in between two parallel morphisms of contexts.

As shown by Gambino and Garner
[
GG08
,
Gar09
]
, the expression
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p(\gamma):a(\gamma)=b(\gamma)
formally verifies the same rules of
Figure

3
verified by the propositional equality and one can simply prove this by sequential (generalised) path induction. In particular, a context elimination (i.e. path induction) rule, with a corresponding context propositional computation rule, is satisfied by the context propositional equality, and moreover every context homotopy equivalence - see
Definition

3.0.1
- is also a context half-adjoint equivalence.

Definition 3.0.1
.

A
context homotopy equivalence
between
\gamma:\Gamma
and
\delta:\Delta
is a couple of morphisms of contexts of the form:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma):\Delta

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{g}}(\delta):\Gamma

such that
there exist
couples of judgements of the form:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=\gamma

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{q}}(\delta):\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))=\delta

hence, as usual, the expression
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma):\Delta
is an abbreviation for the list of judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}_{1}(\gamma):\Delta_{1}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}_{2}(\gamma):\Delta_{2}(\textnormal{{f}}_{1}(\gamma))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}_{3}(\gamma):\Delta_{3}(\textnormal{{f}}_{1}(\gamma),\textnormal{{f}}_{2}(\gamma))

\succ

…

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}_{m}(\gamma):\Delta_{m}(\textnormal{{f}}_{1}(\gamma),\textnormal{{f}}_{2}(\gamma),\textnormal{{f}}_{3}(\gamma),...,\textnormal{{f}}_{m-1}(\gamma))

and the expression
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=\gamma
is an abbreviation for the list of judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}_{1}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}_{1}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=\gamma_{1}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}_{2}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}_{2}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=\textnormal{{p}}_{1}(\gamma)^{*}\gamma_{2}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}_{3}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}_{3}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=(\textnormal{{p}}_{1}(\gamma),\textnormal{{p}}_{2}(\gamma))^{*}\gamma_{3}

\succ

…

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{p}}_{n}(\gamma):\textnormal{{g}}_{n}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=(\textnormal{{p}}_{1}(\gamma),\textnormal{{p}}_{2}(\gamma),...,\textnormal{{p}}_{n-1}(\gamma))^{*}\gamma_{n}
.

In
Section

3.1
we show under what hypotheses these equivalences can be extended to wider contexts.

3.1
Extension of context homotopy equivalences

In what follows, and throughout the entire paper, we will often adopt the following conventions for naming variables: if we denote two given types by
A
and
A^{\prime}
, and denote a variable of type
A
by
x
, then we will often write
\underline{x}
to denote a variable of type
A^{\prime}
. Additionally, we will use
x^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime\prime}
, etc. (and similarly
\underline{x}^{\prime}
,
\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}
, etc.) to denote variables typed by re-indexings of
A
(and of
A^{\prime}
, respectively).

Let
\gamma:\Gamma
and
\delta:\Delta
be contexts and let us assume that we are given a context homotopy equivalence
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
as follows:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma):\Delta

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{g}}(\delta):\Gamma

between them as before. Suppose that we are given the judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A^{\prime}(\delta):\textsc{Type}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f(\gamma,x):A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

such that there are terms of the form:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p(\gamma,x):x=g(\gamma,f(\gamma,x))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;q(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):\underline{x}^{\prime}=f(\gamma,g(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}))
.

In other words, we are given a
homotopy equivalence
(f;g)
between the types in context
A(\gamma)
and
A^{\prime}(\delta)
relative to the context homotopy equivalence
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
. We can use these data in order to augment the context homotopy equivalence
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
to a context homotopy equivalence between the contexts:

\gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)
and
\delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)
.

The remainder of the current subsection is devoted to showing this construction:
the reader who is willing to skip the details may turn to Lemma
3.1.1
.

We observe that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}):A(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))
and that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

hence
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x}):A(g(\delta))
. Let us rename:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{f}}_{m+1}(\gamma,x)\equiv f(\gamma,x):A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})\equiv g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x}):A(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))
.

We observe that:
