8
Conclusion

In this paper we compared a propositional dependent type theory to an extensional one, and observed that, despite the non-negligible weakening of the former with respect to the latter, there is actually an interesting family of judgements where the two theories have the same deductive power.

This result was obtained by adapting the argument introduced by Hofmann
[
Hof96
]
. In spite of the amount of work in the purely syntactic part of this research, namely in the analysis of the family of canonical equivalences, we underline the central and fundamental role of the soundness property of the semantics induced by the class of categories with attributes. The effect is that, for a given term judgement
t:|T|
built by
ETT
, where
T
is an h-elementary type of
PTT
, we do not know how a corresponding term
\tilde{t}:T
of
PTT
(such that
|\tilde{t}|\equiv t
) is defined. A possible future research direction therefore concerns asking whether there is the possibility of making our argument more constructive from this point of view.

Our argument also applies in the case where
PTT
and
ETT
are extended with a (weak) Tarski universe, requiring that it is provably an h-set in
PTT
and that the types associated with its terms are also provably h-sets in
PTT
. Clearly, in this setup, the universe cannot be univalent in
PTT
. To address the issue of accommodating a univalent universe, it would be interesting to explore whether the solution proposed by Winterhalter, Sozeau, and Tabareau
[
WST19
]
, as applied to two-level type theories, can be used to obtain a corresponding adaptation of our argument.

Our proof, strongly based on canonical equivalences, requires that the family of these be restricted, in such a way that further properties are satisfied. The restriction from general contexts to contexts with h-propositional identities and the one from contexts with h-propositional identities to h-elementary contexts are explicitly applied only at very specific points in the proof. However, they are fundamental for defining -by quotienting- an actual category with attributes, i.e. a model of a dependent type theory. From this perspective, it would then be worthwhile to consider what can be learned by replicating our argument without these restrictions. In detail, one might therefore ask whether the argument can be extended, without restrictions on the family of canonical equivalences, in order to deduce a conservativity result for those
generalised type theories
-with less structural rules- that are modelled by the categorical structure obtained -by quotienting- from the general family of canonical equivalences. More generally, we are interested in looking for other ways of applying Hofmann’s argument in order to get similar results or generalised versions of the ones of this paper, depending e.g. on the strictness of the structural rules that we allow in a theory of dependent types.

Appendix A
Proofs of some lemmas

Proof of Proposition
2.1.2
.

The term
a[1_{\Gamma}]
is the unique term of
A[1_{\Gamma}]=A
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A[1_{\Gamma}]=\Gamma.A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\text{[}1_{\Gamma}\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Gamma}.A=1_{\Gamma.A}}

commutes. Hence
a[1_{\Gamma}]=a
, as the square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\Gamma.A}}

commutes.

If we are given morphisms of contexts
\Omega\xrightarrow{g}\Delta\xrightarrow{f}\Gamma
then the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.(A[f])[g]=\Omega.A[fg]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.A[f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta.A[f]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\text{[}f\text{]})\text{[}g\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\text{[}f\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\text{[}f\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g.A\text{[}f\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}

hence in particular, as
(f.A)(g.A[f])=(fg).A
, the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.A[fg]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\text{[}f\text{]})\text{[}g\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(fg).A}

commutes. But
a[fg]
is the unique term of type
A[fg]
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega.A[fg]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\text{[}fg\text{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(fg).A}

commutes, hence
(a[f])[g]=a[fg]
. ∎

Proof of Lemma
2.1.3
.

By building the term
b=b[P_{A}a]
according to the definition of
b[P_{A}a]
itself:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A=\Gamma.A[P_{A}a]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}=P_{A[P_{A}a]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}a]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.A[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A}.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

the upper left-hand square commutes, hence we are done with the first equality. Since
P_{A[P_{A}]}b[P_{A}]a=a
and
(P_{A}.A)(a.A[P_{A}])b=((P_{A}a).A)b=b
, we conclude that
a;b
is the unique arrow
\Gamma\to\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]
whose postcompositions via
P_{A[P_{A}]}
and via
P_{A}.A
are
a
and
b
respectively.

In order to verify that the diagram of the statement commutes, we use the first presentation:

\Delta\xrightarrow{b[f]}\Delta.A[f]\xrightarrow{a[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]}\Delta.A[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]

for
a[f];b[f]
, and the second one:

\Gamma\xrightarrow{a}\Gamma.A\xrightarrow{b[P_{A}]}\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]

for
a;b
. We are left to verify that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A.A[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}(af)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

commutes, since
b[P_{A}(af)]=b[f]
. Since the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b[P_{A}(af)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.A[P_{A}]}

commutes (and this is true because the equality
(a.A[P_{A}])(f.A)=(af).A[P_{A}]
holds) we are done if we verify that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.A[P_{A}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a.A[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f.A.A[P_{A}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]}

commutes and use that
a[f].A[f][P_{A[f]}]=a[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]
. This is actually true as:

(\;f.A.A[P_{A}]\;)(\;a[f].A[P_{A}(f.A)]\;)=(\;(f.A)a[f]\;).A[P_{A}]=(\;af\;).A[P_{A}]=(\;a.A[P_{A}]\;)(\;f.A\;)

hence we are done.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1.3
.

By induction on the complexity of the type
S(\gamma)
.

If
S(\gamma)\equiv S
then by
(a)
the pair:

({\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,s:S\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\varphi(\gamma,s)\equiv s:S;\;{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,s:S\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\psi(\gamma,s)\equiv s:S)

is a canonical homotopy equivalence between
S
and itself relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
. Moreover, let us observe that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,s\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\varphi(\gamma,s)\equiv s=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}s

-by (generalised) path induction on
\alpha(\gamma)
- hence we are done.

If
S(\gamma)
is of the form
\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)
for some judgements
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(\gamma,x):\textsc{Type}
, then by inductive hypothesis there are a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{1}\;|\;g_{1}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\gamma,x):A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,x^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

between
A(\gamma)
and itself relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
such that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x):f_{1}(\gamma,x)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x
and equivalently
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x)=x
and a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{2}\;|\;g_{2}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),y:B(\gamma,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\gamma,x,y):B(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,\underline{y}^{\prime}):B(\gamma,x)

between
B(\gamma,x)
and itself relative to the extension
(\textnormal{{f}},\textnormal{{f}}_{m+1};\textnormal{{g}},\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1})
of
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
via
(f_{1};g_{1})
(Lemma
3.1.1
) such that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x,y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\gamma,x,y)=(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}y
and equivalently:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x,y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}y)=y.

The homotopy equivalence
(f^{\Pi};g^{\Pi})
of Lemma
3.2.1
is a canonical homotopy equivalence relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
. We are left to verify that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,z:\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\Pi}(\gamma,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z)=z.

Let us observe that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,z,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z,f_{1}(\gamma,x))=\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x)=(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)
, where the second equality holds by multiple (generalised) path induction on
(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))
. Hence, by propositional functoriality:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,z,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z,f_{1}(\gamma,x)))=g_{2}(\gamma,x,(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x))=\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)

where the second equality holds by substitution into the judgement:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x,y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}y)=y.

Finally, by propositional functoriality and propositional
\eta
-expansion:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,z\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\Pi}(\gamma,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z)\equiv\lambda x.g_{2}(\gamma,x,\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\gamma)^{*}z,f_{1}(\gamma,x)))=\lambda x.\textnormal{{ev}}(z,x)=z

and we are done.

If
S(\gamma)
is of the form
\Sigma_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)
for some judgements
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(\gamma,x):\textsc{Type}
, then by inductive hypothesis there are a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{1}\;|\;g_{1}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\gamma,x):A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,x^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

between
A(\gamma)
and itself relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
such that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x):f_{1}(\gamma,x)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x
and equivalently
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x)=x
and a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{2}\;|\;g_{2}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),y:B(\gamma,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\gamma,x,y):B(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,\underline{y}^{\prime}):B(\gamma,x)

between
B(\gamma,x)
and itself relative to the extension
(\textnormal{{f}},\textnormal{{f}}_{m+1};\textnormal{{g}},\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1})
of
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
via
(f_{1};g_{1})
(Lemma
3.1.1
) such that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x,y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\gamma,x,y)=(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}y
and equivalently:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x,y\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x))^{*}y)=y.

The homotopy equivalence
(f^{\Sigma};g^{\Sigma})
of Lemma
3.3.1
is a canonical homotopy equivalence relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
, hence we are left to verify that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,u:\Sigma_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\Sigma}(\gamma,u)\equiv\langle f_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u),f_{2}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u,\pi_{2}u)\rangle=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}u.

As
\alpha_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u):f_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}\pi_{1}u
and:

f_{2}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u,\pi_{2}u)=(\alpha(\gamma),\alpha_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u))^{*}\pi_{2}u=\alpha_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u)^{*}(\alpha(\gamma)^{*}\pi_{2}u)

-where the last equality follows by (generalised) path induction on
\alpha_{1}(\gamma,\pi_{1}u)
- it is in fact the case that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,u\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\Sigma}(\gamma,u)=\langle\alpha(\gamma)^{*}\pi_{1}u,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}\pi_{2}u\rangle=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}\langle\pi_{1}u,\pi_{2}u\rangle=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}u

where the second equality follows by multiple (generalised) path induction on
\alpha(\gamma)
and the third by propositional functoriality and propositional
\eta
-expansion.

If
S(\gamma)
is of the form
s_{1}(\gamma)=s_{2}(\gamma)
for some judgement
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
, then by inductive hypothesis there is a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f\;|\;g\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f(\gamma,x):A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g(\gamma,x^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

between
A(\gamma)
and itself relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
such that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x):f(\gamma,x)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x
and equivalently
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x)=x
. For
i=1,2
let us observe that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\gamma,s_{i}(\gamma)):f(\gamma,s_{i}(\gamma))=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}s_{i}(\gamma)\textnormal{ and }{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;s_{i}(\alpha(\gamma)):s_{i}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}s_{i}(\gamma)

hence:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;r_{i}(\gamma)\equiv\alpha_{1}(\gamma,s_{i}(\gamma))\bullet s_{i}(\alpha(\gamma))^{-1}:f(\gamma,s_{i}(\gamma))=s_{i}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

and then the homotopy equivalence
(f^{=},g^{=})
of Lemma
3.4.1
is a canonical homotopy equivalence relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
. We are left to show that
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,p:s_{1}(\gamma)=s_{2}(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{=}(\gamma,p)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}p
. In fact:

\displaystyle{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,p\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{=}(\gamma,p)

\displaystyle=s_{1}(\alpha(\gamma))\bullet\alpha_{1}(\gamma,s_{1}(\gamma))^{-1}\bullet f(\gamma,p)\bullet\alpha_{1}(\gamma,s_{2}(\gamma))\bullet s_{2}(\alpha(\gamma))^{-1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}(\alpha(\gamma))\bullet\alpha(\gamma)^{*}p\bullet s_{2}(\alpha(\gamma))^{-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}p

by propositional groupoidality and since
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma,x\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\gamma,x):f(\gamma,x)=\alpha(\gamma)^{*}x
. Here the last of these equalities follows by multiple (generalised) path induction on
\alpha(\gamma)
and by propositional groupoidality.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1.5
.

By induction on the complexity of
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
.

If
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
is of the form
(a)
then we are done.

If
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
is of the form
(b)
then
S(\gamma)\equiv\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)
and
T(\delta)\equiv\Pi_{\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)}B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})
for some judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(\gamma,x):\textsc{Type}

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A^{\prime}(\delta):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}):\textsc{Type}

and there are a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{1}\;|\;g_{1}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\gamma,x):A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

between
A(\gamma)
and
A^{\prime}(\delta)
relative to
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
and a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;f_{2}\;|\;g_{2}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),y:B(\gamma,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\gamma,x,y):B^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),\underline{y}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,\underline{y}^{\prime}):B(\gamma,x)

between
B(\gamma,x)
and
B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})
relative to the extension
(\textnormal{{f}},\textnormal{{f}}_{m+1};\textnormal{{g}},\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1})
of
(\textnormal{{f}};\textnormal{{g}})
via
(f_{1};g_{1})
(Lemma
3.1.1
), in such a way that the homotopy equivalence
(f^{\Pi};g^{\Pi})
of Lemma
3.2.1
is the given
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
. By inductive hypothesis there are a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;g^{\prime}_{1}\;|\;f^{\prime}_{1}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}):A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,x^{\prime}):A^{\prime}(\delta)

between
A^{\prime}(\delta)
and
A(\gamma)
relative to
(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime})
and a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;g^{\prime}_{2}\;|\;f^{\prime}_{2}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),\underline{y}:B^{\prime}(\delta,x)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\prime}_{2}(\delta,\underline{x},\underline{y}):B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\prime}_{2}(\delta,\underline{x},y^{\prime}):B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})

between
B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})
and
B(\gamma,x)
relative to the extension
(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime},\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}_{n+1};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime},\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime}_{m+1})
of
(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime})
via
(g^{\prime}_{1};f^{\prime}_{1})
, in such a way that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}):g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x})

i.e.
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\alpha(\delta)^{*}x^{\prime})=\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}f^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,x^{\prime})

and that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),\underline{y}:B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}\underline{y})=(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x},\underline{y})

i.e.
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}y^{\prime})=(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}f^{\prime}_{2}(\delta,\underline{x},y^{\prime})

where we remind that:

\displaystyle\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})

\displaystyle\equiv g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})

\displaystyle\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})

\displaystyle\equiv q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})^{-1}

in context
\delta,\underline{x}
, being
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;q_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):\underline{x}^{\prime}=f_{1}(\gamma,g_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}))
. Then
\langle\;g^{\prime\Pi}\;|\;f^{\prime\Pi}\;\rangle
is a canonical homotopy equivalence between
\Pi_{\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)}B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})
and
\Pi_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)
and relative to
(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime})
, by
(b)
. We are left to verify that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta,z^{\prime}:\Pi_{x^{\prime}:A(g^{\prime}(\delta))}B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),x^{\prime})\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\Pi}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\alpha(\delta)^{*}z^{\prime})=\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}f^{\prime\Pi}(\delta,z^{\prime}).

Let us observe that:

\displaystyle f^{\prime\Pi}(\delta,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta).f_{2}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x},\textnormal{{ev}}(z^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})))

\displaystyle=\lambda\underline{x}.(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{-1})^{*}q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})^{*}f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}\textnormal{{ev}}(z^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})))

\displaystyle=\lambda\underline{x}.(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{-1})^{*}q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})^{*}f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\delta)^{*}z^{\prime},g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),q(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})))

in context
\delta,z^{\prime}
, where the first propositional equality follows by:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;

{f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}y^{\prime})=(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}f^{\prime}_{2}(\delta,\underline{x},y^{\prime})}

and the second by:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}):g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}).

Therefore:

\displaystyle q(\delta)^{*}f^{\prime\Pi}(\delta,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=q(\delta)^{*}\lambda\underline{x}.(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{-1})^{*}q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})^{*}f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\delta)^{*}z^{\prime},\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})))

\displaystyle=\lambda\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))).q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\underline{x}^{\prime\prime})^{*}f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}),\textnormal{{ev}}(\alpha(\delta)^{*}z^{\prime},g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\underline{x}^{\prime\prime})))

\displaystyle\equiv f^{\Pi}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\alpha(\delta)^{*}z^{\prime})

in context
{\delta,z^{\prime}}
, where the first propositional equality follows by propositional
\Pi
-extensionality and propositional functoriality and the second by multiple (generalised) path induction on
q(\delta)
. We are done.

If
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
is of the form
(c)
then as before
S(\gamma)\equiv\Sigma_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)
and

for some judgements:

\succ

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B(\gamma,x):\textsc{Type}

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A^{\prime}(\delta):\textsc{Type}
and
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}):\textsc{Type}

\langle\;f_{1}\;|\;g_{1}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\gamma,x):A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):A(\gamma)

between

and

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,x:A(\gamma),\underline{y}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma),f_{1}(\gamma,x))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\gamma,x,\underline{y}^{\prime}):B(\gamma,x)

:

between

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}):g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x})

i.e.
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\alpha(\delta)^{*}x^{\prime})=\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}f^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,x^{\prime})

and that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),\underline{y}:B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}\underline{y})=(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x},\underline{y})

i.e.
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),y^{\prime}:B(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta),g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}y^{\prime})=(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}f^{\prime}_{2}(\delta,\underline{x},y^{\prime})

where, as before, we remind that:

\displaystyle\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})

\displaystyle\equiv g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})

\displaystyle\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x})

\displaystyle\equiv q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})^{-1}

in context
\delta,\underline{x}
, being
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\textnormal{{f}}(\gamma))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;q_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}):\underline{x}^{\prime}=f_{1}(\gamma,g_{1}(\gamma,\underline{x}^{\prime}))

\langle\;g^{\prime\Sigma}\;|\;f^{\prime\Sigma}\;\rangle

and
\Sigma_{x:A(\gamma)}B(\gamma,x)

(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime})

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta,\underline{u}:\Sigma_{x^{\prime}:A^{\prime}(\delta)}B^{\prime}(\delta,x^{\prime})\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\Sigma}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime\Sigma}(\delta,\underline{u}).

Let us observe that:

\displaystyle{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta,\underline{u}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}):\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u})

\displaystyle=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u})

\displaystyle{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta,\underline{u}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}))^{*}\pi_{2}\underline{u})

\displaystyle=(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}))^{*}g_{2}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u},\pi_{2}\underline{u})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u})^{*}\alpha(\delta)^{*}g_{2}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u},\pi_{2}\underline{u})

where the first equality in the second judgement follows by:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta),\underline{y}:B^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}\underline{y})=(\alpha(\delta),\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}))^{*}g^{\prime}_{1}(\delta,\underline{x},\underline{y})

and the second by (generalised) path induction on
\alpha_{1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u})
. Therefore:

\displaystyle\langle\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),

\displaystyle g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}))^{*}\pi_{2}\underline{u})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\alpha(\delta)^{*}g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),\alpha(\delta)^{*}g_{2}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u},\pi_{2}\underline{u})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(\delta)^{*}\langle g_{1}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),g_{2}^{\prime}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u},\pi_{2}\underline{u})\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime\Sigma}(\delta,\underline{u})

in context
{\delta,\underline{u}}
, where the second propositional equality follows by multiple (generalised) path induction on
\alpha(\delta)
. Finally:

\displaystyle g^{\Sigma}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u})

\displaystyle\equiv\langle g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\pi_{1}\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u}),g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),g_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\pi_{1}\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u}),q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\pi_{1}\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u})^{*}\pi_{2}\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),g_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{g}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}),(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}_{n+1}(\delta,\pi_{1}\underline{u}))^{*}\pi_{2}\underline{u})\rangle

in context
{\delta,\underline{u}}
, where the propositional equality follows by firstly applying multiple (generalised) path induction on
\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)
and secondly by (generalised) path induction on
q_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\pi_{1}\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{u})
. We are done.

Notation for the last section of the proof.
Whenever:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \omega:\Omega\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;x(\omega),y(\omega):O(\omega)

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \omega\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha:x(\omega)=y(\omega)

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \omega,\omega^{\prime}:\Omega\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;p:\omega^{\prime}=\omega

we denote as
p^{*}\alpha:x(\omega^{\prime})=y(\omega^{\prime})
the usual transport of the term
\alpha(\omega)
from the type
x(\omega)=y(\omega)
to the type
x(\omega^{\prime})=y(\omega^{\prime})
. However, the operation
p^{*}
is defined on the terms of
O(\omega)
as well, hence the terms
p^{*}x(\omega),p^{*}y(\omega):O(\omega^{\prime})
are defined. Therefore the operation
p^{*}
might be extended as usual to a propositional functor from
O(\omega)
to
O(\omega^{\prime})
and a term
p^{*}\alpha:p^{*}x(\omega)=p^{*}y(\omega)
is defined. In the last section, in order to distinguish between the terms:

p^{*}\alpha:x(\omega^{\prime})=y(\omega^{\prime})
and
p^{*}\alpha:p^{*}x(\omega)=p^{*}y(\omega)

that we usually indicate by the same notation, we indicate the
latter
as
\underline{p^{*}\alpha}
. Observe that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ p:\omega^{\prime}=\omega\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x(\omega^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y(\omega^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}x(\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}y(\omega).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{p^{*}\alpha}}

In order to verify this, since
\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}\alpha=\alpha
and since
x(\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime}))
and
y(\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime}))
are propositionally equal to the canonical proofs
\xi(x(\omega^{\prime}))
and
\xi(y(\omega^{\prime}))
that
x(\omega^{\prime})=\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}x(\omega^{\prime})
and
y(\omega^{\prime})=\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}y(\omega^{\prime})
and by path induction, it is enough to verify that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \omega\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x(\omega^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y(\omega^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}x(\omega^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}y(\omega^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi(x(\omega^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi(y(\omega^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\textnormal{{r}}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}\alpha}}

which is true because
\xi(-)
is a homotopy.

If
\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
is of the form
(d)
then
S(\gamma)\equiv s_{1}(\gamma)=s_{2}(\gamma)
and

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \gamma:\Gamma\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A(\gamma):\textsc{Type}

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;A^{\prime}(\delta):\textsc{Type}

in such a way that the homotopy equivalence

of Lemma

\langle\;\varphi\;|\;\psi\;\rangle
. By inductive hypothesis there is a canonical homotopy equivalence
\langle\;g^{\prime}\;|\;f^{\prime}\;\rangle
:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x}):A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f^{\prime}(\delta,x^{\prime}):A^{\prime}(\delta)

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{x}:A^{\prime}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha_{1}(\delta,\underline{x}):g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{x})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{x})

i.e.
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,x^{\prime}:A(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;f(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\alpha(\delta)^{*}x^{\prime})=\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}f^{\prime}(\delta,x^{\prime})
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let us observe that in context
\delta
:

\alpha_{1}(\delta,t_{i}(\delta))^{-1}:\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,t_{i}(\delta))=g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}t_{i}(\delta))

g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{i}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta))^{-1}):g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}t_{i}(\delta))=g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{i}(\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))))

g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1}):g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{i}(\textnormal{{f}}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))))=g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),f(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))))

p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))):g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),f(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))))=s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))

s_{i}(\alpha(\gamma)):s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))=\alpha(\delta)^{*}s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))

and let us call
a_{i}(\delta):\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,t_{i}(\delta))=\alpha(\delta)^{*}s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))
. Let
r_{i}^{\prime}(\delta):g^{\prime}(\delta,t_{i}(\delta))=s_{i}(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime}(\delta))
be such that:

\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}r_{i}^{\prime}(\delta)}=a_{i}(\delta).

Hence
\langle\;g^{\prime=}\;|\;f^{\prime=}\;\rangle
is a canonical homotopy equivalence between
t_{1}(\delta)=t_{2}(\delta)
and
s_{1}(\gamma)=s_{2}(\gamma)
relative to
(\textnormal{{g}}^{\prime};\textnormal{{f}}^{\prime})
, by

, and we are left to verify that:

{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta:\Delta,\underline{p}:t_{1}(\delta)=t_{2}(\delta)\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;g^{=}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p})=\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime=}(\delta,\underline{p})

that is in fact true, as
{\bm{\lfloor}}\penalty\ \delta,\underline{p}\penalty\ {\bm{\rfloor}}\;\;\;\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime=}(\delta,\underline{p})\equiv

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha(\delta)^{*}(r_{1}^{\prime}(\delta)^{-1}\bullet g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{p})\bullet r_{2}^{\prime}(\delta))

\displaystyle=s_{1}(\alpha(\delta))\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}(r_{1}^{\prime}(\delta)^{-1}\bullet g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{p})\bullet r_{2}^{\prime}(\delta))}\bullet s_{2}(\alpha(\delta))^{-1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}(\alpha(\delta))\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}r_{1}^{\prime}(\delta)^{-1}}\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{p})}\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}r_{2}^{\prime}(\delta)}\bullet s_{2}(\alpha(\delta))^{-1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}(\alpha(\delta))\bullet a_{1}(\gamma)^{-1}\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{p})}\bullet a_{2}(\gamma)\bullet s_{2}(\alpha(\delta))^{-1}

\displaystyle=p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{1}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)))\bullet\alpha_{1}(\delta,t_{1}(\delta))\bullet\underline{\alpha(\delta)^{*}g^{\prime}(\delta,\underline{p})}\;\bullet

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(\delta,t_{2}(\delta))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{2}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

\displaystyle=p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{1}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)))\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\underline{\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p}})\;\bullet

\displaystyle g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{2}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

\displaystyle=p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),t_{1}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta))\bullet\underline{\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p}}\bullet t_{2}(\textnormal{{q}}(\delta))^{-1})\;\bullet

\displaystyle g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

\displaystyle=p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p})\;\bullet

\displaystyle g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

\displaystyle=p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))^{-1}\bullet g(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),r_{1}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))\bullet\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p}\bullet r_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta))^{-1})\bullet p(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),s_{2}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta)))

\displaystyle\equiv g^{=}(\textnormal{{g}}(\delta),\textnormal{{q}}(\delta)^{*}\underline{p})

hence we are done.∎
